  • Statement. The KTerm tsp-meta builds for the substitution lemma — if Gamma,x:S ⊢ e:T and Gamma ⊢ v:S then Gamma ⊢ [v/x]e:T — satisfies check_type(ctx, proof.term, proof.goal) = Ok(()) where proof.goal = substitution_statement(lang).
  • Why. Preservation's beta/elimination step is unprovable without it; it is the named crux of the spine. Its proof structure mirrors tsp_term::subst/shift exactly — an off-by-one in shift (INV-3) manifests here as a proof term the kernel cannot type. So INV-7 is the proof-side mirror of INV-3.
  • Enforced where. tsp-meta builds the term; tsp_kernel::check_type is the sole arbiter. Proof { goal, term } with term : goal.
  • Detection. tsp check runs check_type over it; CI gate fails on Err. Regression: any edit to subst/shift re-runs this proof.
  • Telemetry. proof_check{lemma="substitution"} = Ok/Err, proof_term_nodes, kernel_check_us.
  • Recovery. None — a failing crux proof blocks the whole metatheory layer.
  • Debugging. KERNEL_TRACE=1 localizes the conversion (equal) or Pi-application step that fails; correlate the failing index with the shift arithmetic in INV-3.

7.8 INV-8 — Preservation + Progress proof terms type-check (CRITICAL)

  • Statement. check_type(ctx, P_pres.term, preservation_statement(lang)) = Ok and check_type(ctx, P_prog.term, progress_statement(lang)) = Ok, where the goals are exactly:
    • Preservation: ⊢e:T ∧ e-->e' ⇒ ⊢e':T (subject reduction).
    • Progress: ⊢e:T ⇒ value(e) ∨ ∃e'. e-->e'.
  • Why. These two are the project's deliverable theorems. Acceptance by the kernel is the machine-checked guarantee of type soundness (their corollary: well-typed terms never get stuck, Phase 10).
  • Enforced where. tsp-meta constructs the terms (preservation via inversion + INV-7; progress via canonical_forms); tsp_kernel::check_type arbitrates. Both depend transitively on INV-7.
  • Detection. tsp check gate; both MUST be Ok for the language and its extensions (naturals/booleans/products/sums in Phase 10, references/System F in Phase 12). Conformance suite (Phase 13) pins them.
  • Telemetry. proof_check{thm="preservation"|"progress"}, proof_term_nodes, end-to-end tsp_check_total_ms.
  • Recovery. None — binary; a failing theorem means the language (or a lemma) is wrong, surfaced before any release.
  • Debugging. Decompose: re-check the lemmas (canonical_forms, inversion, weakening, substitution) individually; the first failing lemma localizes the defect. KERNEL_TRACE=1 on the failing branch.

7.9 INV-9 — Generator emits only well-typed closed terms (CRITICAL)

  • Statement. Every Term from Generator::next() is closed and satisfies infer(empty_ctx, e) = Ok(_). The generator is type-directed (builds terms by a target type), so ill-typed outputs are impossible by construction.
  • Why. The Redex-style tester's value proposition is: a property failure is a real soundness bug, not a generator bug. If the generator could emit ill-typed terms, a check_preservation failure would be ambiguous. INV-9 makes every counterexample trustworthy.
  • Enforced where. tsp_test::Generator (size-bounded, type-directed); a debug-mode post-condition debug_assert!(infer(&e).is_ok()) on each yielded term.
  • Detection. Property meta-test: draw N=10^6 terms, assert infer(e)=Ok for all (the generator's own test suite, tsp-test/tests/gen_welltyped.rs). Any Err fails the build.
  • Telemetry. gen_terms_total, gen_reject_total (must be 0), term-size histogram, type-shape coverage (Arrow/Prod/Sum/Ref/All proportions).
  • Recovery. A bad output is checked in as a generator regression fixture; build fails until the generator is fixed.
  • Debugging. Re-run with the failing RNG seed (recorded per draw) to reproduce; shrink to the minimal ill-typed term and inspect the generation rule that produced it.

7.10 INV-10 — Broken rule rejected by kernel AND falsified by tester (dual-guard, CRITICAL)

  • Statement. For the deliberately broken typing rule (Phase 12), both guards fire: (a) the corresponding preservation/progress proof term fails check_type (kernel rejects); (b) the tester returns TestResult::CounterExample(e, Witness) with e shrunk minimal (tester falsifies). Conversely, for the sound language, the tester returns only Pass(n) and both proofs check_type = Ok.
  • Why. The dual-guard design is the spine's central demonstration: proofs and property-testing are complementary, and a real bug trips both. A bug caught by only one guard reveals a gap in the other (e.g. an untested type shape, or a too-weak Prop).
  • Enforced where. tsp-meta (proof side) + tsp-test (check_progress/check_preservation, Shrinker). The broken-rule variant lives as a fixture language .lang.
  • Detection. Phase 12/13 conformance: assert kernel Err and tester CounterExample for the broken variant; assert kernel Ok and tester Pass for the sound variant. Cross-validation test that the two guards agree on the verdict per language.
  • Telemetry. dual_guard{variant, kernel=Ok|Err, tester=Pass|CE}; alert if the two disagree.
  • Recovery. Disagreement (one guard misses the bug) is itself a defect — strengthen the missing guard before the gate passes.
  • Debugging. If the tester misses it: inspect generator coverage (INV-9 telemetry) for the unexercised type shape. If the kernel "misses" it (accepts the bad proof): escalate to INV-1 — a TCB soundness breach.

7.11 INV-11 — whnf/equal decide definitional equality soundly (GRAVEST)

  • Statement. equal(ctx,a,b) = true ⟺ a ≡ b definitionally (beta, optionally eta); whnf(t) is the weak-head normal form of a well-typed t. No over-conversion (equating non-equal types — would let INV-1 accept ill-typed proofs) and no under-conversion (failing to equate truly-equal types — would reject valid proofs, breaking INV-7/8).
  • Why. Conversion is the engine of check_type's mode-switch (infer then equal). Over-conversion is a soundness hole inside the TCB (hence GRAVEST, a facet of INV-1); under-conversion is a completeness bug.
  • Enforced where. tsp_kernel::whnf,equal. equal typically whnf-reduces both sides then compares heads, recursing structurally (with eta-expansion at Pi if eta enabled).
  • Detection. Property tests: reflexivity, symmetry, transitivity of equal; equal(t, whnf(t)) = true; beta law equal(App(Lam(a,b),x), subst(b,0,x)) = true; a negative corpus of definitionally-distinct KTypes that MUST return false. Fuzz (INV-2) also exercises equal for termination.
  • Telemetry. equal_true_total,equal_false_total,whnf_steps histogram, equal_us p99.
  • Recovery. None — binary; an over-conversion bug is a TCB freeze event (see INV-1 recovery).
  • Debugging. Dump the two whnf reduction sequences side-by-side (KERNEL_TRACE=1); the first differing head reveals over/under-conversion.

7.12 INV-12 — TCB boundary minimality (CRITICAL)

  • Statement. The trusted computing base is exactly tsp-kernel. No soundness-deciding logic exists in tsp-syntax/-term/-rules/-meta/-test/-cli; those crates may be buggy without compromising a checked proof, because the kernel re-validates every proof term. tsp-kernel: ≤ 800 LOC, #![forbid(unsafe_code)], deps ⊆ {core,alloc}.
  • Why. Soundness is only as trustworthy as the TCB is small + audited. A large or leaky TCB is unauditable; a "helpful" optimization in tsp-meta that pre-decides a sub-goal moves trust outside the kernel and voids INV-1's guarantee.
  • Enforced where. Crate dependency graph (tsp-kernel depends on nothing internal); architecture review; a CI check on cargo tree -p tsp-kernel (no extra edges) + a LOC gate (tokei tsp-kernel/src ≤ 800).
  • Detection. CI: dependency-graph assertion, LOC ceiling, forbid(unsafe_code) compile gate, clippy deny-list (INV-2). Manual line-by-line audit logged at each Phase 13 gate.
  • Telemetry. tcb_loc, tcb_unsafe_count (must be 0), tcb_dep_count (must be 0 internal).
  • Recovery. Any soundness logic found outside tsp-kernel is refactored back in (or deleted) before merge; LOC overruns trigger a kernel-simplification task.
  • Debugging. cargo tree/cargo modules to find an illicit dependency; review tsp-meta for any decision not routed through check_type.

7.13 INV-13 — step determinism + type-respecting (MAJOR)

  • Statement. step(e) is a partial function (deterministic: at most one reduct, via fixed evaluation contexts / left-to-right congruence); step(e)=None ⟺ e is a value or stuck. On closed well-typed e, step(e)=Some(e') ⇒ infer(e')=infer(e) (the executable shadow of preservation) and step(e)=None ⇒ is_value(e) (the shadow of progress).
  • Why. Determinism makes preservation provable by induction on the (unique) step; the type-respecting shadows are cheap runtime sanity checks that catch rule-table edits before the heavier kernel proofs run.
  • Enforced where. tsp_rules::step with explicit evaluation contexts.
  • Detection. Property test: for closed well-typed e, step is single-valued (compare against an all-redexes enumerator → must be ≤1); infer(step(e)?) == infer(e); step(e)=None ⇒ is_value(e). This is the tester's check_preservation/check_progress at the executable level, complementing INV-8/INV-10.
  • Telemetry. step_total, stuck_total (must be 0 on well-typed corpus), step_value_total.
  • Recovery. A stuck well-typed term ⇒ the rule set is unsound; fix the rules and re-run INV-8 proofs.
  • Debugging. Shrink to the minimal closed well-typed term that gets stuck or changes type; inspect the evaluation context chosen.

7.14 INV-14 — Shrinker preserves the falsified property (MAJOR)

  • Statement. If Shrinker reduces counterexample e to e', then e' still falsifies the same property (!check_progress(e') or !check_preservation(e')) and e' remains well-typed (INV-9). Shrinking is monotone toward a minimal failing term.
  • Why. A counterexample is only useful if minimal; a shrinker that drops the failure (returns a passing term) or an ill-typed term produces a misleading CounterExample, undermining INV-10's debugging value.
  • Enforced where. tsp_test::Shrinker; each shrink step re-checks the property + infer.
  • Detection. Property test: along any shrink path, the property stays violated and infer=Ok; the terminal e' has no smaller failing neighbor.
  • Telemetry. shrink_steps histogram, shrink_size_reduction ratio, shrink_invalid_total (must be 0).
  • Recovery. A property-dropping shrink step is a shrinker bug; constrain the shrink moves and re-run.
  • Debugging. Log the shrink trajectory (sizes + property verdict per step); the step that first restores Pass is the faulty move.

7.15 Invariant dependency lattice

            INV-1  Kernel soundness (GRAVEST, TCB)
            /  |  \
       INV-2 INV-11 INV-12        (totality, conversion, TCB minimality)
              |
       INV-7  Substitution-lemma proof  <-- mirrors -->  INV-3 shift/subst (term core)
              |                                              |
       INV-8  Preservation + Progress proofs            INV-4 alpha_eq
              |                                              |
       INV-10 Dual-guard (broken rule)  <-- agrees -->  INV-9 Generator well-typed
              |                                              |
              +----------- INV-5 check_deriv  INV-6 infer  INV-13 step  INV-14 Shrinker
  • Reading. INV-1 is the root; if it fails, every downstream "checked" guarantee is void. INV-3 (computational subst) and INV-7 (its proof-side mirror) are a matched pair — the spine's stated off-by-one trap lives across this edge. INV-9 ⊥ INV-10: the tester's worth depends on well-typed generation. INV-12 keeps INV-1 auditable; a violated INV-12 makes INV-1 unverifiable even if technically true.
  • Gate. Phase 13 ("done") requires: INV-1,2,11 fuzzed clean (0 crashes/timeouts/false-accepts); INV-7,8,10 proofs Ok for sound languages and the broken-rule variant rejected by both guards; INV-3,4,5,6,9,13,14 property suites green; INV-12 LOC + dependency gates met.

8. Domain Model — Object Calculi, Judgments, and the Proof Kernel

The domain has two strata: the object level (textbook calculi — STLC + extensions — whose soundness we prove, lives in tsp-term/tsp-rules) and the meta level (the lambda-Pi logical framework in tsp-kernel that checks the proofs about the object level, glued by tsp-meta). Every object-level artifact (term, type, typing judgment, derivation, lemma) is reflected into a KTerm/KType and re-validated by the trusted kernel. This section pins the grammars, the index arithmetic, the inference rules, the metalanguage that defines a calculus, the kernel's own type theory, the propositions-as-types bridge, and the worked examples that fix semantics with zero ambiguity.

8.1 Object Syntax — EBNF (STLC + locked extensions)

Concrete surface grammar parsed by tsp-syntax into tsp-term::{Term, Type}. Greek written ASCII; -> is the type arrow, --> is reduction (never in surface syntax).

Type  ::= Atom                          (* Base(Atom): Bool, Nat, ... *)
        | Type "->" Type                (* Arrow; right-assoc *)
        | Type "*"  Type                (* Prod *)
        | Type "+"  Type                (* Sum *)
        | "Ref" Type                    (* Ref; references stretch *)
        | "All" "." Type                (* All; System F stretch, binder over TyVar *)
        | TyIdx                         (* TyVar(usize); de Bruijn type var *)
        | "(" Type ")"
Term  ::= Ident                         (* surface name; elaborated to Var(index) *)
        | "\\" ":" Type "." Term        (* Lam(Type, body); 1 binder *)
        | Term Term                     (* App; left-assoc *)
        | "true" | "false"              (* TmTrue / TmFalse *)
        | "if" Term "then" Term "else" Term      (* If *)
        | "0"                           (* Zero *)
        | "succ" Term                   (* Succ *)
        | "(" Term "," Term ")" | "fst" Term | "snd" Term     (* Prod ctors/dtors *)
        | "inl" Term | "inr" Term | "case" Term "of" Term "|" Term  (* Sum *)
        | "ref" Term | "!" Term | Term ":=" Term              (* Ref stretch *)
        | "(" Term ")"

Precedence (tight→loose): application > succ/ref/!/fst/snd/inl/inr > * > + > -> > \./if/case. Atom = interned identifier (a u32 symbol; Type::Base / Judgment::form reuse the same intern table).

8.2 De Bruijn Representation — Index Arithmetic (the computational core)

Var(n) = the binder n levels out; n counts intervening Lam (term) binders (and All for TyVar). Lam binds index 0 in its body; subst/shift are the exact arithmetic the substitution lemma must mirror — an off-by-one here breaks Phase 7's machine-checked proof.

shift(t, d, c) adds d (may be negative) to every free index (index >= c); c (cutoff) counts binders crossed so it never touches bound indices.

shift(Var n, d, c)        = Var n               if n <  c     (bound -> unchanged)
                          = Var (n + d)         if n >= c     (free  -> shifted; n+d >= 0 required)
shift(Lam ty b, d, c)     = Lam ty (shift(b, d, c+1))         (entering binder bumps cutoff)
shift(App f a, d, c)      = App (shift f d c) (shift a d c)
shift(If c1 t e, d, c)    = If (shift c1 ..) (shift t ..) (shift e ..)   (* every subterm, same c *)
shift(Succ e, d, c)       = Succ (shift e d c)                (* TmTrue/TmFalse/Zero: no recursion *)

subst(t, j, s) = [s/j] t: replace Var j by s, with s shifted up by the binder depth so its free variables stay free under the binders of t.

subst(Var n, j, s)        = s                   if n == j
                          = Var n               otherwise
subst(Lam ty b, j, s)     = Lam ty (subst(b, j+1, shift(s, 1, 0)))   (j bumped; s shifted under binder)
subst(App f a, j, s)      = App (subst f j s) (subst a j s)
(structural recursion through If/Succ/pairs/sums identically, j unchanged at non-binders)

Beta-reduction (the redex (\:T.b) v) uses the standard substitute-then-shift-down idiom so the consumed binder's slot vanishes:

beta(Lam _ b, v)  =  shift( subst(b, 0, shift(v, 1, 0)), -1, 0 )

alpha_eq(a,b) is then structural == on the Term trees (de Bruijn makes alpha-equivalence syntactic — no name comparison; capture-avoidance is automatic given correct shift). is_value(t): Lam | TmTrue | TmFalse | Zero | Succ(v if is_value v) | (v1,v2) | inl v | inr v (+ Ref locations in the stretch).

8.2.1 Worked substitution example (capture-avoidance via shift)

Reduce (\:Bool. \:Bool. 1) true — outer \ binds index 0, inner body Var 1 refers *past* the inner binder to the outer one. Must yield a term where the surviving free reference is preserved, **not** captured by the inner \.

Reducing the redex App (Lam Bool (Lam Bool (Var 1))) TmTrue: beta sets b = Lam Bool (Var 1) and v = TmTrue. Since TmTrue carries no free variables, shift(v,1,0) leaves it unchanged. Substituting into b, entering the inner Lam bumps the target index to j=1; Var 1 matches exactly and is replaced, giving Lam Bool TmTrue. The closing down-shift shift(_,-1,0) has no free index left to lower, so the result is Lam Bool TmTrue — the constant-true function of type Bool -> Bool.

Contrast the naive (no-shift) bug: substituting a v containing Var 0 into Lam's body without shift(v,1,0) would let the inner binder capture it — exactly the defect the substitution lemma forbids and the kernel proof would fail to discharge.

8.3 Typing Rules — Gamma |- e : T (inference-rule notation)

Gamma = de Bruijn Context (a Vec<Type>, index 0 = innermost binding). Gamma |- e : T is an inductive Judgment with form = Has-Type. tsp-rules::infer is the executable, syntax-directed reading; check_deriv validates an explicit Derivation tree against these Rules.

            x:T in Gamma  (Gamma[x] = T)
T-Var       ----------------------------                 (de Bruijn lookup at index x)
                  Gamma |- Var x : T

            Gamma, S |- e : T
T-Abs       --------------------------                   (push S; body typed under extended ctx)
            Gamma |- Lam S e : S -> T

            Gamma |- f : S -> T     Gamma |- a : S
T-App       ---------------------------------------
                    Gamma |- App f a : T

T-True   ------------------- Gamma |- true : Bool      T-False ----- ... |- false : Bool

            Gamma |- c : Bool   Gamma |- t : T   Gamma |- e : T
T-If        ----------------------------------------------------
                       Gamma |- If c t e : T

T-Zero  --------------- Gamma |- 0 : Nat        T-Succ  (Gamma |- e:Nat) / Gamma |- Succ e : Nat

            Gamma |- e1 : S   Gamma |- e2 : T            Gamma |- p : S * T
T-Pair      -------------------------------    T-Fst    -------------------    (T-Snd symmetric: snd p : T)
            Gamma |- (e1,e2) : S * T                     Gamma |- fst p : S

            Gamma |- e : S                               Gamma |- s : S+T  Gamma,S |- l:R  Gamma,T |- r:R
T-Inl       ---------------------   (T-Inr -> S+T)  T-Case ----------------------------------------------
            Gamma |- inl e : S + T                                Gamma |- case s of l | r : R

Refs stretch (Phase 12) adds a store typing Sigma (judgment Gamma | Sigma |- e : T): T-Ref : (..|-e:T)/ ref e : Ref T, T-Deref : (..|-e:Ref T)/ !e : T, T-Assign : (..|-e1:Ref T, ..|-e2:T)/ e1:=e2 : Unit. System F stretch adds T-TAbs/T-TApp over All/TyVar with type-level shift/subst.

8.4 Small-Step Semantics — e --> e', Values, Evaluation Contexts

Call-by-value, left-to-right. tsp-rules::step(e) -> Option<Term> (None = value-or-stuck). Computation rules (redexes) + congruence via evaluation contexts E.

Values v ::= Lam S e | true | false | 0 | succ v | (v,v) | inl v | inr v

E ::= []                                       (* the hole *)
    | E a | v E                                (* App: function before argument *)
    | If E t e
    | succ E | (E, a) | (v, E) | fst E | snd E
    | inl E | inr E | case E of l | r          (* + ref E | ! E | E := a | v := E  (stretch) *)

           e --> e'
E-Ctx   --------------                         (* congruence: reduce inside the unique hole *)
        E[e] --> E[e']

E-AppAbs   (App (Lam S b) v) --> beta(Lam S b, v)            (v a value; see 8.2 beta)
E-IfTrue   If true  t e --> t        E-IfFalse  If false t e --> e
E-FstPair  fst (v1,v2) --> v1        E-SndPair  snd (v1,v2) --> v2
E-CaseInl  case (inl v) of l|r --> beta(l, v)    E-CaseInr  case (inr v) of l|r --> beta(r, v)

E decomposition is unique for any non-value closed e (the redex position is determined) → step is deterministic (optional Phase 4 theorem step is a partial function). A closed, well-typed e is either a value or E[redex] with a matching computation rule — this is progress (Section 8.5(ii) / Phase 9). A stuck term is a non-value with no rule (e.g. If 0 t e, fst true) — soundness states these are unreachable from well-typed terms.

8.5 The Metalanguage — LangDef (defining a calculus as data)

A .lang file declares a calculus; tsp-syntax parses it into LangDef { sorts, grammar, judgments, rules }. This is how an object language is specified once and consumed by every downstream crate (checker, stepper, kernel encoder, tester).

LangDef    ::= { SortDecl | Production | JudgmentForm | RuleDef }
SortDecl   ::= "sort" Atom { "," Atom }                       (* -> Vec<Atom> sorts: Term, Type, Ctx *)
Production ::= "grammar" Atom "::=" Alt { "|" Alt }           (* -> Production; per-sort grammar *)
Alt        ::= Ctor { SortRef | "(" Binder ")" }
Binder     ::= "binds" Atom "in" SortRef                      (* binding annotation -> de Bruijn arity *)
JudgmentForm ::= "judgment" Atom "(" SortRef { "," SortRef } ")"   (* e.g. Has-Type(Ctx,Term,Type) *)
RuleDef    ::= "rule" Atom ":" { Judgment } "----" Judgment   (* premises ---- conclusion *)

Maps directly onto the locked types:

.lang construct parses to consumed by
sort LangDef.sorts: Vec<Atom> scope/arity checks in tsp-syntax
grammar + binds LangDef.grammar: Vec<Production> de Bruijn elaboration in tsp-term
judgment LangDef.judgments: Vec<JudgmentForm> Judgment.form validation
rule .. ---- .. LangDef.rules: Vec<Rule> (premises,conclusion) check_deriv; kernel encoding

ParseErrorKind: UnexpectedToken, UnknownSort (sort used before sort-declared), BadBinder (binds over a non-binding position / arity mismatch), MalformedRule (no ----, or conclusion form not a declared judgment). The locked STLC .lang declares sort Term, Type, Ctx; the grammar of 8.1; judgment Has-Type(Ctx,Term,Type) and judgment Step(Term,Term); and the rules of 8.3–8.4.

8.6 The Proof Kernel — lambda-Pi (LF) Core: Grammar + Typing

The trusted computing base. A dependently-typed lambda-Pi calculus with two sorts Type : Kind (Sort::Type, Sort::Kind). Prop = KType, Proof = KTerm; "valid" means check_type accepts. Target TCB size <= 800 LOC (Section targets / Phase 13 gate).

KTerm s,t,A,B ::= Var n            (de Bruijn, into Ctx)
                | Sort Type | Sort Kind
                | Pi A B           (dependent function type; B under one binder)   (* Box<KTerm>,Box<KTerm> *)
                | Lam A b          (abstraction; domain A, body b under binder)
                | App f a
Ctx Gamma     ::= .  |  Gamma, A           (de Bruijn list of KTerm "types"; shift on lookup)

KType is the subset of KTerm inhabiting a Sort. Kernel shift/subst on KTerm are the same de Bruijn arithmetic as 8.2 (one binder per Pi/Lam). Bidirectional checker: infer_type synthesizes, check_type checks against an expected type (drives App argument checking and Lam under a Pi).

            Gamma(n) = A
K-Var       --------------                      (de Bruijn context lookup, shifted)
            Gamma |- Var n : A

K-Type   ------------------------ Gamma |- Sort Type : Sort Kind        (Type : Kind, no Kind : _)

            Gamma |- A : Sort s1     Gamma, A |- B : Sort s2
K-Pi        --------------------------------------------------         (s1,s2 in {Type,Kind}; PTS-style)
                     Gamma |- Pi A B : Sort s2

            Gamma, A |- b : B
K-Lam       ---------------------------                                (well-formed Pi presupposed)
            Gamma |- Lam A b : Pi A B

            Gamma |- f : Pi A B     Gamma |- a : A
K-App       ----------------------------------------                   (result = subst(B, 0, a) up to shift)
                  Gamma |- App f a : [a/0] B

            Gamma |- t : A     Gamma |- B : Sort s     A == B  (defeq)
K-Conv      ---------------------------------------------------         (the conversion rule)
                          Gamma |- t : B

A == B in K-Conv is equal(ctx, A, B) — definitional equality, not syntactic. K-App's dependency [a/0]B is what makes propositions-as-types expressive (a typing judgment indexed by a term).

8.6.1 whnf + Definitional Equality

whnf(t): reduce only the head redex until the outermost former is exposed (a Var/Sort/Pi/Lam head, or a stuck App). One rule — head beta — applied repeatedly; no reduction under binders or in arguments (that is the weak, head discipline → cheap, terminating on well-typed terms).

On an App f a head, whnf recurses on f; if that yields a Lam _ b, it fires the head redex (beta) and continues from the result, otherwise it stops with the stuck application App (whnf f) a. Var/Sort/Pi/Lam heads are already in weak-head normal form and return unchanged.

equal(ctx,a,b) = type-directed / structural conversion with beta (and optional eta): both sides are first reduced to whnf, then compared head-by-head — Vars by index equality, Sorts by equality, Pi A B/Pi A' B' by equal-ing domains and (under the shared binder) codomains, Lam A b/Lam A' b' likewise on domain and body, and two stuck Apps by equal-ing both the head and the argument. If eta is enabled, a Lam compared against a non-Lam g is equal when b equals App (shift g 1 0) (Var 0) (the eta-expansion of g). Any other head shape mismatch is unequal.

Decidability rests on whnf termination for well-typed KTerms; equal is the only place conversion happens, so it is small and heavily audited (a bug here = unsoundness — see 8.8).

8.7 Propositions-as-Types — Encoding Judgments & Derivations (Curry-Howard)

tsp-meta reflects the object level into the kernel: each object Judgment becomes a KType (an LF type family indexed by encoded terms), each Rule becomes a Pi-typed constructor, each Derivation becomes a KTerm proof term. The logical reading:

Logic / metatheory lambda-Pi former kernel API
implication P => Q Pi P (shift Q) (non-dependent Pi) K-Pi
universal forall x:A. P Pi A P (dependent) K-Pi
atomic prop `Gamma - e : T` KType: App(App(App HasType g) e) t
conjunction P /\ Q encoded pair type (Church/sigma) derived
a Rule's validity Pi-chain: premises → conclusion progress_statement/preservation_statement
a Derivation applied constructors = a KTerm check_type
"proof is valid" check_type(ctx, term, goal) = Ok the kernel

Locked statements (Phases 8–9), as KTerm props built by tsp-meta:

preservation_statement(lang)  ==  Pi e:Term. Pi T:Type. Pi e':Term.
                                    HasType nil e T  ->  Step e e'  ->  HasType nil e' T
progress_statement(lang)      ==  Pi e:Term. Pi T:Type.
                                    HasType nil e T  ->  ( Value e  \/  Exists e':Term. Step e e' )

Proof { goal: KTerm, term: KTerm }: the proof obligation is discharged iff check_type(&Ctx::empty(), &proof.term, &proof.goal) == Ok(()). The object-level Rules of 8.3–8.4 become the inductive type family constructors; tsp-meta also emits the eliminator / induction principle for Has-Type (the dependent recursor) — this is what the preservation/progress proof terms apply to do induction on the typing derivation (8.8(ii)).

8.8 Worked Examples

(i) Typecheck + step a concrete STLC term

(\:Bool. if 0 then false else true) true — apply the boolean-negation function to true.

Let term = App (Lam Bool (If (Var 0) TmFalse TmTrue)) TmTrue. Typing by T-App needs both subterms: the Lam pushes Bool onto the context and, inside, T-If types Var 0 as Bool (T-Var lookup) with both branches Bool, so the Lam has type Bool -> Bool; TmTrue types as Bool by T-True. Matching S -> T = Bool -> Bool against argument type S = Bool yields result T = Bool. Stepping term fires E-AppAbs (the argument TmTrue is a value): the beta result is shift(subst(If (Var 0) TmFalse TmTrue, 0, TmTrue), -1, 0) = If TmTrue TmFalse TmTrue, which itself steps by E-IfTrue to the value TmFalse (step then returns None).

infer before and after gives Bool (preservation holds on this trace); final term is a value (progress terminated correctly).

(ii) Canonical forms + a slice of preservation, as a kernel-checked proof term

Canonical forms (Bool): if |- v : Bool and Value v then v in {true,false}. As a KType and its proof:

canon_bool : Pi v:Term. HasType nil v Bool -> Value v -> ( Eq v TmTrue \/ Eq v TmFalse )

Its proof term abstracts over v, the typing witness, and the value witness, then applies the Has-Type eliminator to that pair — inversion leaves only the T-True/T-False constructor cases live, each directly yielding the disjunct (invalid constructors at Bool, e.g. from If or App, are ruled out because no Rule concludes Has-Type _ _ Bool for them at a value).

Preservation slice — the E-AppAbs case (where the substitution lemma is invoked). The proof term applies the typing eliminator; at the beta step it feeds the substitution lemma subst_lemma (Phase 7) — itself a kernel term mirroring tsp-term::subst:

subst_lemma : Pi G:Ctx.Pi S:Type.Pi T:Type.Pi e:Term.Pi v:Term.
                 HasType (cons S G) e T -> HasType G v S -> HasType G (subst e 0 v) T

For the E-AppAbs case, inversion of T-App recovers f = Lam S b with HasType nil (Lam S b) (S->T) and HasType nil v S; the preservation proof term applies subst_lemma to the inverted abstraction typing and the argument typing to produce HasType nil (subst b 0 v) T — exactly the type of beta (Lam S b) v. check_type(&empty, pres_appabs_term, preservation_statement) returns Ok(()) iff the index arithmetic in the stated subst matches the kernel's subst — i.e. the proof type-checks exactly when tsp-term::subst is correct. This is the load-bearing coupling.

(iii) Redex-style counterexample for a DELIBERATELY broken rule

Break T-App to ignore the argument type (the classic POPLmark-style bug):

T-App-BAD    Gamma |- f : S -> T     Gamma |- a : ANY      (* premise "a : S" dropped *)
             ------------------------------------------
                      Gamma |- App f a : T

tsp-test::Generator produces a well-typed-under-BAD term; check_preservation/check_progress falsify it; Shrinker minimizes:

The Generator (size ≤ 4) yields a well-typed-under-BAD candidate such as App (Lam Bool (If (Var 0) ..)) Zero — Zero : Nat fed where the (unchecked) argument position expected Bool. check_progress finds it neither a value nor able to step: step reduces to If Zero TmFalse TmTrue, which is STUCK (no E-If rule matches a Nat guard), so the property is FALSE. The Shrinker reduces this to the minimal witness App (Lam Bool (Var 0)) Zero, reported as TestResult::CounterExample with a Witness naming the failed property (Progress) and the stuck subterm.

Dual guard: simultaneously, tsp-meta cannot build a checking proof term under T-App-BAD — progress_statement's proof needs canonical_forms at Bool, but Zero is not a Bool canonical form, so check_type returns Err (the proof fails to check). The kernel and the tester agree the language is unsound (Phase 12).

8.9 Malformed Examples → Exact Errors

Input Layer Error Why
Var 3 in empty Context (ill-scoped index) tsp-rules::infer TypeError::UnboundVar(3) de Bruijn index >= Gamma.len(); no T-Var lookup
object program with free name x (no binder) tsp-syntax elab ParseError{ kind: UnexpectedToken } → unbound cannot assign a de Bruijn index
.lang rule with no ---- separator tsp-syntax ParseErrorKind::MalformedRule premises/conclusion split absent
.lang judgment Has-Type using sort Trm (typo) tsp-syntax ParseErrorKind::UnknownSort sort not in LangDef.sorts
binds x in Type on a non-binding ctor position tsp-syntax ParseErrorKind::BadBinder binding arity mismatch
Derivation citing rule T-App but conclusion shape is T-Abs tsp-rules::check_deriv DerivError::ConclusionMismatch tree node's conclusion not unifiable with Rule.conclusion
Derivation with wrong premise count check_deriv DerivError::ArityMismatch subderivations.len() != rule.premises.len()
ill-typed proof: App f a with f : Pi A B, a : C, A != C tsp-kernel::check_type KernelError::TypeMismatch{ expected:A, got:C } K-App argument check fails; equal(A,C)=false
proof term App (Sort Type) a tsp-kernel::infer_type KernelError::NotAFunction(Sort Type) head of App has no Pi type
Lam A b where A is not a type (A : Kind-less) tsp-kernel::infer_type KernelError::NotAType K-Lam/K-Pi domain must inhabit a Sort
adversarial cyclic-looking KTerm (deep App nest) tsp-kernel::whnf bounded; returns or KernelError::Diverged well-typed inputs terminate; ill-typed are rejected, never panic (Phase 13 fuzz invariant)

Invariant (gravest failure class): infer_type/check_type must never return Ok for an ill-typed KTerm and must never panic on adversarial input — accepting one bad proof term silently validates a false theorem and collapses the entire project's guarantee. Every malformed object-level artifact is caught at its own layer; anything that slips through is the kernel's last-line responsibility, which is why the TCB is kept <= 800 LOC and audited (8.6, Phase 13).

9. Tech Stack ADR Table

Architecture Decision Records for project 284. One row per locked decision; superseding rows note the ADR they replace. Status Accepted unless noted. The load-bearing decisions are ADR-04 (proof-term checking, not search), ADR-06 (tiny audited TCB), and ADR-13 (dual assurance) — these define the soundness story.

ADR # Decision Locked Choice Rejected Alternatives Rationale When the Rejected Choice Wins
ADR-01 Implementation language + edition Rust, edition 2021, MSRV 1.78 OCaml/Haskell; C/C++; reuse Coq/Lean/Agda Kernel is a hot, recursion-heavy checker where one soundness bug invalidates every proof → memory safety + exhaustive match on Term/Type/KTerm/Derivation is decisive. de Bruijn → usize; shift/subst total fns the borrow checker keeps honest. Haskell laziness complicates the strict kernel; C/C++ unsafe term graphs in the TCB unacceptable; reusing a prover defeats the from-scratch goal (that is #280). 1.78 = floor for let-else, OnceLock, edition-2021 captures; no nightly in the build. OCaml/Haskell wins when the object language is itself functional and the metatheory is a direct GADT or data-type proof that can be extracted to compiled code (e.g., a certified compiler like CompCert). Reusing Coq/Lean wins when the team's goal is the fastest-to-formal-proof path and "from-scratch" is not a learning objective.
ADR-02 Workspace decomposition Seven crates: tsp-syntax, tsp-term, tsp-rules, tsp-kernel, tsp-meta, tsp-test, tsp-cli One monolithic crate; two crates (lib+bin); fewer merged crates (e.g. fold tsp-meta into tsp-kernel) Crate boundaries make the TCB auditable: tsp-kernel compiles with zero deps on tsp-meta/tsp-test/tsp-cli, so "what must be trusted" = one crate. Dependency DAG cli → {meta,test} → {rules,kernel} → {term,syntax} (kernel depends only on core/alloc). Monolith hides the trust boundary; merging meta into kernel would let proof-construction code touch the checker's internals. A monolith wins when the whole codebase is trusted equally (no trust boundary to enforce) and compile times matter more than auditability — e.g., a research prototype where every line is reviewed before any commit. Fewer crates win when the team is one person and the proof layer is small enough to fold into the kernel without exceeding the LoC budget.
ADR-03 Object-term binding representation de Bruijn indices (Var(usize)); shift(t,d,cutoff), subst(t,j,s), structural alpha_eq Named variables + capture-avoiding rename; locally-nameless (named free / de Bruijn bound); HOAS / PHOAS; co-de-Bruijn alpha-equivalence becomes structural (alpha_eq = == on the tree) → no rename, no freshness oracle, no capture by construction. Substitution lemma proof then mirrors tsp-term::subst/shift line-for-line — the crux invariant (ADR-12). Named binding needs gensym + capture checks (subtle, untestable boundary). Locally-nameless adds a second sort of variable + open/close bureaucracy for no metatheory payoff at STLC scale. HOAS leaks the meta-binder into the object language and blocks alpha_eq as data. Locally-nameless wins for mechanized System F-sub metatheory (POPLmark) where human-readable proofs matter and the open/close overhead is justified by proof ergonomics. HOAS/PHOAS wins when the meta-language is a dependently-typed proof assistant (Coq, Agda) and α-renaming is handled by the host binder — irrelevant in a standalone Rust kernel. Named variables win for a pedagogical interpreter where variable names are the primary debugging surface.
ADR-04 Proof discipline of the kernel Proof-term CHECKING — a lambda-Pi / LF core that type-checks a supplied proof term (infer_type/check_type) Tactic-based interactive proof; automated proof SEARCH; SMT-backed checking Curry-Howard: Prop = KType, Proof = KTerm; "valid" ⇔ check_type(ctx, term, goal) accepts. Checking is small, total, decidable, and auditable; search/tactics need a large engine in the TCB and are explicit NON-GOALS (#280 = interactive prover, #278 = proof search/SMT). The framework's deliverable is checked metatheory, not discovered metatheory. Tactic-based proof wins when the team's bottleneck is authoring proof terms for large inductive proofs (e.g., Preservation over a 10-former language with 50+ cases) — a tactic engine reduces per-case boilerplate by generating proof terms automatically. SMT-backed checking wins when the propositions are quantifier-free arithmetic or bit-vector predicates where automated search is complete and the prover is trusted independently.
ADR-05 Logical framework / kernel calculus lambda-Pi (LF, à la Edinburgh LF / LambdaPi): KTerm = Var|Sort|Pi|Lam|App, Sort = Type|Kind, Type : Kind Calculus of (Inductive) Constructions; pure System F-omega; first-order logic + sequent rules; HOL lambda-Pi is the minimal dependently-typed core that expresses judgments-as-types and Pi (= dependent implication / forall) — exactly what STLC metatheory needs (Harper-Honsell-Plotkin, Twelf, LambdaPi tutorial). CIC is far larger than the proof obligations warrant (bigger TCB). FOL/HOL can't internalize derivation trees as terms. Two-universe Type:Kind (no Kind:Kind) keeps it predicative-enough and consistent for this use; no Kind:Kind (Girard's paradox) — locked. CIC (Coq's kernel) wins when the object language requires strong eliminators for data types (e.g., induction over natural numbers inside the proof term), or when the metatheory includes impredicative Prop. System F-omega wins when the metatheory stays purely prenex-polymorphic and no dependent types are needed. HOL wins when automation is the priority and the judgments do not need to internalize derivation trees.
ADR-06 Trusted Computing Base size + isolation TCB = tsp-kernel only, target ≤ 1500 LoC (stretch ≤ 1000), no external deps beyond core/alloc Trust the whole workspace; trust tsp-rules (executable checker) as authoritative; allow std in the kernel Soundness of the entire project rests on the kernel: GRAVEST failure = kernel accepts an ill-typed KTerm (silently validates a false theorem). Smaller + dependency-free ⇒ humanly auditable + fuzzable (ADR-19). tsp-rules::infer is convenience, NOT trusted (a bug there yields a stuck checker, not a false theorem). no_std + alloc forbids accidental I/O / clock / RNG inside the trust boundary. Trusting the whole workspace wins only when every line of code is formally verified or manually reviewed at the same rigor as the kernel — a realistic posture for a solo researcher but not for a team. Allowing std in the kernel wins when the kernel must perform I/O (e.g., a kernel-as-a-service with a verified transport layer). Trusting tsp-rules wins when the executable checker has itself been machine-verified (e.g., via a separate Coq proof of infer's soundness).
ADR-07 Kernel checking algorithm Bidirectional infer_type / check_type Full type inference (Hindley-Milner / unification-based); checking-only (no infer mode); elaboration with metavariables Bidirectional is the standard minimal-TCB design (LambdaPi tutorial): infer synthesizes for eliminators/vars, check for introductions, switching at the annotation/App boundary. Full inference for dependent types is undecidable and needs unification + metavariables = large, error-prone TCB. Annotations on Lam/Pi are cheap (proof terms are generated by tsp-meta, not hand-written), so demanding them costs nothing and shrinks the kernel. Elaboration with metavariables wins when the proof language has a human-facing surface syntax where explicit annotations are ergonomically unacceptable (e.g., a user-facing proof assistant like Lean 4, where implicit arguments drive the experience). Full inference wins when the language is simply-typed and HM is complete — relevant for the object language's infer in tsp-rules, but not for the kernel's dependent Pi-types.
ADR-08 Definitional equality / conversion equal(ctx,a,b) via whnf — beta + alpha; eta optional (feature-gated eta) Full normalization-by-evaluation (NbE) then syntactic ==; untyped beta only, no whnf sharing; eta always-on Conversion only needs enough reduction to compare heads, so whnf-then-recurse-on-spine is minimal and lazy (avoids normalizing under unreached binders). Eta makes equal heavier and its termination argument subtler → default OFF, behind eta feature, enabled only where a proof needs it; documented in the TCB audit. Full NbE is more machinery in the TCB than STLC metatheory conversions require. Conversion is part of trust — kept tight. NbE wins when the kernel must check intensional type equality under function type in a system with large proofs of functional extensionality — the canonical NbE advantage. Eta-always-on wins when the object language encodes everything via function types and proof terms regularly produce eta-expanded forms (common in the CIC tradition). Untyped beta only wins for a checked-proof pipeline where all proof terms are already in beta-normal form on entry.
ADR-09 Judgment encoding into the kernel Shallow LF-style judgments-as-types (object Has-Type/Step as kernel inductive signatures + eliminators) Deep embedding (object syntax/derivations as a kernel Inductive datatype with a well_formed predicate); axiomatic/postulated lemmas LF tradition: an object judgment is a type family, an inference Rule is a constant, a Derivation is a term — adequacy is immediate and the kernel checks tree validity for free (Twelf-style). Deep embedding duplicates Term/Derivation inside the kernel and forces a re-proved check_deriv in the TCB. Axioms are forbidden: every lemma is a checked term, never postulate (would defeat soundness). Deep embedding wins when the object language is itself a dependently-typed language and the metatheory requires induction over a data type (not just a type family) — the standard choice in Coq developments for languages with binders (e.g., PLFA). Axiomatic/postulated lemmas win in a prototype phase where proof-term authoring is deferred and the goal is to test whether the statement is the right one before committing to a full proof.
ADR-10 Executable artifacts beside the relations Ship executable infer/step (tsp-rules) AND check_deriv next to the inductive Rules Relations only (no executable checker); executable checker as the sole authority (no proofs) Executable infer/step/check_deriv give an oracle to test against (Phase 3/4) and feed tsp-test's generator, but they are explicitly NON-trusted (ADR-06): correctness of the metatheory comes from tsp-meta proofs, not from infer. Relations-only loses the testable oracle + the CLI typecheck/step; checker-only loses the machine-checked guarantee. We keep both and pin which is authoritative. Relations-only wins when the project is purely a formal proof development with no user-facing CLI (e.g., a Coq contribution to a proof library). Executable-only wins when fast development iteration is the priority and the team plans to add proofs later — acceptable for a prototype, not for the Phase 13 "done" gate.
ADR-11 Reduction-order + semantics style Small-step, call-by-value, evaluation-context / congruence formulation; step(e) -> Option<Term> (None = value/stuck) Big-step / natural semantics; small-step call-by-name; abstract machine (CEK/CK) Small-step is the form progress + preservation are stated/proved over (TAPL, Software Foundations); call-by-value matches the references stretch (a store needs CBV order) and canonical-forms reasoning. Evaluation contexts factor congruence cleanly and prep the Redex layer (ADR-16). Big-step can't state progress (no "stuck" notion); CBN complicates the references extension; an abstract machine is more state than the metatheory needs. Determinism of step proved (optional, Phase 4). Big-step wins for a language where type safety is stated via logical-relation termination arguments (Girard's method for System F) rather than the progress+preservation decomposition. Call-by-name wins for a lazy language where step models Haskell/GHC semantics. An abstract machine (CEK) wins when the implementation target is an efficient bytecode interpreter and the metatheory is proved separately via a certified compiler correctness argument.
ADR-12 shift/subst as total functions shift/subst are pure, total, panic-free fns; the substitution lemma proof mirrors them exactly Partial subst returning Result; in-place mutation; memoized/interned term sharing in v1 The substitution lemma (Gamma,x:S ⊢ e:T ∧ Gamma ⊢ v:S ⇒ Gamma ⊢ [v/x]e:T) is the crux; an off-by-one in shift breaks it (and only it). Totality means the proof and the code share one recursion structure → a tsp-test differential check can pin them together. Result-subst would force error-handling into the lemma statement. Interning is a later optimization (ADR-21) gated on benchmarks, never in v1 (sharing bugs in the TCB-adjacent core are not worth it early). In-place mutation wins when subst is the inner loop of an efficient kernel that must avoid allocation pressure (e.g., a production type-inference engine processing millions of terms per second) and all subst call sites have been proved safe by a prior analysis. Memoized sharing wins at Phase 13 benchmark time, after the correctness gate, once criterion data shows allocation pressure is the bottleneck — exactly the ADR-21 sequencing.
ADR-13 Assurance model Dual guard: kernel-checked proofs (tsp-meta) + Redex-style property tests (tsp-test) Proofs only; tests only; proofs + manual review only Proofs give certainty for the modeled language; randomized/exhaustive tests give fast falsification + confidence the model matches the executable infer/step. A correct language ⇒ tester only Pass; a broken rule MUST be caught by BOTH (kernel: proof no longer checks; tester: shrunk counterexample) — the dual-guard contract (ADR-14). Tests catch modeling bugs proofs can't see (e.g. encoder ≠ infer); proofs catch what finite testing misses. Neither alone is sufficient. Proofs-only wins when the proof development is done in a full proof assistant where the executable checker is extracted from the proof (CompCert model) — then the checker and the proof are the same artifact. Tests-only wins for a language design phase where the calculus is changing rapidly and investing in full proofs would be invalidated each iteration.
ADR-14 Broken-rule demonstration as a first-class artifact A deliberately unsound typing rule is a checked-in fixture; CI asserts kernel proof FAILS and tsp-test returns a shrunk CounterExample Document the failure only; only the tester demonstrates it; only the kernel demonstrates it Makes the dual guard executable and regression-proof (Phase 12): a "negative" conformance test that both pillars react. The proof must fail to type-check (not error spuriously) and the tester must produce a minimal witness via the Shrinker. Doc-only / single-pillar can't prove the guards actually fire; this is the load-bearing demonstration that the framework detects unsoundness. Documentation-only wins when the unsoundness is too subtle for a finite tester to find in the size budget (e.g., a bug that only appears at term depth ≥ 1000) and the proof failure is the only practical detector. Tester-only wins for a rule that is computationally obvious but formally complex to disprove — a useful intermediate state during proof development.
ADR-15 Generator / shrinker design Type-directed, size-bounded generator of well-typed Terms + a structural Shrinker (TestResult = Pass(u64)|CounterExample(Term, Witness)) Untyped term generator + filter-by-typecheck; grammar-only random terms; no shrinker Type-directed generation (build a term for a goal type) yields a high density of well-typed terms (filtering untyped terms is exponentially wasteful at depth). Size bound caps blow-up + makes runs reproducible (seeded). The Shrinker reduces a failing Term to a minimal counterexample (smaller subterms / constructor collapse), and Witness records which property failed (progress vs preservation) + the offending step — essential for diagnosing the broken rule (ADR-14). An untyped generator wins when the soundness property being tested is syntactic (e.g., parser round-trip, pretty-printer correctness) and any term — typed or not — is a valid input. Grammar-only terms win for a surface-syntax fuzzer targeting parse robustness, not semantic properties. No-shrinker wins only in a continuous fuzzing regime where a crash corpus is replayed and minimized offline by an independent tool.
ADR-16 Property semantics tested Exactly the two soundness properties: check_progress(e) (closed well-typed ⇒ value ∨ steps) and check_preservation(e) (well-typed ∧ step ⇒ same type) Ad-hoc invariants; round-trip parse/print only; confluence/normalization These are the theorems tsp-meta proves, so the tester checks the same statements ⇒ direct cross-validation (Phase 11: tester and proofs agree on the sound language). Round-trip/confluence are off-target (confluence/normalization not claimed; STLC normalizes but it is a NON-GOAL to prove here). Tester reuses the executable infer/step as oracle (ADR-10). Confluence testing wins when the operational semantics is nondeterministic (e.g., concurrent/interleaved semantics) and the safety property is confluence of reduction paths rather than single-step preservation. Normalization testing wins when the language is known terminating and the goal is measuring reduction cost distributions rather than soundness.
ADR-17 Error-handling contract Result<_, KernelError|TypeError|DerivError|ParseError> everywhere; kernel NEVER panics and NEVER accepts an ill-typed term; #![deny(clippy::unwrap_used, clippy::panic, clippy::expect_used)] in tsp-kernel panic!/unwrap on malformed input; Option without error reason; exceptions-as-control-flow Two locked kernel invariants: no panic on adversarial KTerm (a panic = DoS, found by fuzzing ADR-19) and no false accept (the soundness invariant). Errors carry a kind (ParseErrorKind::{UnexpectedToken,UnknownSort,BadBinder,MalformedRule}, KernelError, …) for diagnostics + conformance assertions. The clippy denies make "the kernel cannot unwrap" a compile-time guarantee, not a code-review convention. Panic-on-invalid wins in a setting where all inputs are pre-validated by a trusted layer before reaching the kernel (e.g., an embedded RTOS where a panic triggers a safe system reboot rather than arbitrary behavior). Option-only wins for a minimal pedagogical implementation where error messages are irrelevant and the focus is the core algorithm.
ADR-18 Reference oracle / ground truth Pin behavior to TAPL + Software Foundations: STLC rules, progress/preservation statements, references + System F as in TAPL; POPLmark as a scope yardstick Invent bespoke rules; derive semantics ad hoc; match one textbook loosely The metatheory must be recognizably correct: rule shapes, the canonical-forms lemma, and the progress/preservation statements follow TAPL chs. 8–9, 13, 23 and Software Foundations' mechanized versions verbatim, so reviewers can diff against the literature. POPLmark (System F-sub binding/metatheory) sets the adequacy bar for the binding representation (ADR-03). Bespoke rules would make "is this the right theorem?" unanswerable. Bespoke rules win when the object language is a novel calculus (e.g., a new effect system or capability type system) with no prior mechanization — the "from-scratch metatheory" use case where the project is the reference, not a downstream consumer of existing literature.
ADR-19 Fuzzing cargo-fuzz (libFuzzer) targets on tsp-kernel (infer_type, whnf, equal) + tsp-syntax parser; assert no panic & no false-accept; corpus checked in No fuzzing; only unit tests; AFL (extra toolchain) The two kernel invariants (ADR-17) are exactly fuzzing targets: feed arbitrary/structured KTerms and assert never panics and never accepts ill-typed (cross-checked: if infer_type returns Ok(ty) then check_type(_, t, ty) must also accept — internal consistency). cargo-fuzz is the idiomatic Rust harness (no extra toolchain vs AFL). Differential fuzzing also pins infer(rules) vs the kernel encoding. Phase 13 gate. AFL wins when the target runs in a language/environment where cargo-fuzz/libFuzzer integration is unavailable (e.g., a kernel module, a C extension, or a Rust no_std binary that cannot link libFuzzer's runtime). No-fuzzing wins only for a short-lived prototype where the threat model does not include adversarial inputs and the deployment is air-gapped.
ADR-20 Benchmarking criterion micro-benchmarks: kernel proof-check time, whnf/equal cost, proof-term size, tsp-test throughput; tracked vs targets #[bench]/nightly; ad-hoc Instant timing; no benchmarks criterion gives stable statistics on stable Rust (no nightly, per ADR-01) with regression detection. Locked targets (Phase 13): kernel check of the STLC preservation+progress proof < 50 ms; whnf amortized O(spine); proof-term size for STLC soundness ≤ ~5k KTerm nodes; tsp-test ≥ 10k well-typed terms/s; kernel peak memory < 64 MB on the largest checked proof. Numbers are the "done" gate, not vanity. #[bench]/nightly wins in a development environment where nightly Rust is already required (e.g., a project using other nightly features) and the overhead of pulling in criterion is undesirable. Ad-hoc Instant timing wins for a one-off profiling session where reproducibility across runs is irrelevant and a single wall-clock sample is the only goal.
ADR-21 Performance-optimization policy Correctness-first: no interning, no NbE, no caching in v1; optimizations are post-benchmark + must not enlarge the TCB Premature interning/hash-consing of KTerm; eager full normalization caches; arena allocation in the kernel from day 1 A sharing/caching bug inside the TCB can cause a false accept (ADR-06) — far costlier than slowness. So Box-based trees + naive whnf ship first; any optimization (e.g. interned KTerm, memoized whnf) is justified by a criterion regression (ADR-20) and re-fuzzed (ADR-19). The kernel stays small/auditable over fast. Hash-consing from day 1 wins when the performance target is known in advance to be unachievable without sharing (e.g., a production LF kernel checking million-node proof terms in a CI pipeline), and the hash-consing layer is itself formally verified or small enough to audit. Arena allocation wins for a kernel embedded in a time-critical real-time system where heap fragmentation is a safety concern.
ADR-22 Dependency policy (esp. the TCB) tsp-kernel/tsp-term/tsp-syntax = no_std + alloc, ZERO external crates; non-TCB crates: dev-deps only (criterion, proptest-style, cargo-fuzz); strict cargo-deny (licenses, advisories, bans) Liberal use of serde, parser/proptest crates as normal deps; macro-heavy frameworks; FFI Every dependency in the TCB is code you must trust unsoundly-or-not → the kernel takes none. Outside the TCB, test/bench/fuzz deps are dev-dependencies (never linked into tsp release / never into tsp-kernel). cargo-deny in CI bans new transitive deps, denies copyleft, fails on RUSTSEC advisories. Keeps the audit surface and supply-chain risk minimal. Liberal dependencies win when the kernel is already independently verified (e.g., by a proof of its Rust code in Verus or Creusot), so additional crate dependencies cannot introduce undetected unsoundness. serde as a normal dep wins when the primary interface to the kernel is a persistent proof store that must serialize/deserialize KTerm values across process boundaries.
ADR-23 CI pipeline GitHub Actions: fmt --check → clippy -D warnings → cargo test --workspace → conformance suite → cargo-deny → fuzz-smoke (bounded) → criterion regression; matrix {MSRV 1.78, stable}; Dockerfile-pinned toolchain Local-only checks; single-toolchain CI; no conformance/fuzz in CI Soundness is a gate, not a hope: CI must run the conformance suite (proofs check; broken variants rejected; tester ⇔ proofs agree — ADR-14/16), a bounded fuzz smoke (regression on the checked-in corpus), and benchmark thresholds. MSRV-vs-stable matrix prevents accidental newer-feature use (ADR-01/24). Dockerfile pins the exact toolchain so the TCB builds bit-reproducibly for audit. Local-only checks win in an offline or air-gapped environment where CI infrastructure is unavailable. Single-toolchain CI wins for a project with a single consumer who controls both the compiler and the deployment environment and can tolerate manual MSRV verification.
ADR-24 MSRV stability guarantee MSRV 1.78 pinned in rust-toolchain.toml + asserted in CI; bumping MSRV is itself an ADR Track latest stable; allow nightly features; unpinned toolchain A trusted-base project must build on a fixed, audited compiler; silent MSRV creep changes the compiler the TCB's soundness implicitly relies on. Pinning + a CI MSRV job makes any required-feature bump an explicit, reviewed decision (new ADR), not drift. No nightly ⇒ no feature(...) in the TCB. Tracking latest stable wins when the project depends on a rapidly-evolving standard library API (e.g., a systems crate for a new hardware platform) and MSRV pinning would require maintaining backports. Nightly features win when the project is a Rust compiler plugin or internal tooling that already requires nightly for procedural macros or ABI reasons.
ADR-25 Licensing MIT OR Apache-2.0 (dual), per-crate license field + LICENSE-MIT/LICENSE-APACHE at workspace root GPL/AGPL; single MIT; proprietary/unlicensed Rust-ecosystem default dual license = maximal downstream reuse + patent grant (Apache-2.0) while staying permissive (MIT). Copyleft would deter use as a metatheory dependency and is denied by cargo-deny (ADR-22). Education/reference-oriented project (TAPL/Software-Foundations lineage) ⇒ permissive is the right posture. GPL/AGPL wins when the goal is ensuring that all downstream improvements to the kernel are contributed back to the public (e.g., an academic project funded with a requirement that derived tools remain open). Proprietary wins when the kernel is a commercial component of a verified-software toolchain where the trust boundary must be contractually owned rather than community-reviewed.

10. Repository Layout

Single Cargo workspace tsp/. Seven crates under crates/; each maps 1:1 to a spine member. Directory split mirrors the TCB boundary: tsp-kernel is physically isolated (own deny.toml allow-list, own fuzz target, own audit doc) because a kernel bug silently validates a false theorem (gravest failure class). Phase-to-path mapping is explicit (right column) so reviewers locate the artifact for any phase heading ## 12..## 25.

10.5 Layout invariants

Invariant Enforcement
tsp-kernel (the TCB) has zero external deps crate-local deny.toml empty allow-list; CI fails on any dep
TCB size <= ~1500 lines scripts/tcb-count.sh in ci.yml; budget tracked in tsp-kernel/AUDIT.md
tsp-term, tsp-syntax, tsp-kernel, tsp-meta are #![no_std] + extern crate alloc lib.rs attribute + clippy in CI
#![forbid(unsafe_code)] workspace-wide (mandatory in kernel) per-lib.rs + main.rs
Dependency edges acyclic, exactly: term←{syntax,rules}; {term,syntax}←rules; {rules,kernel}←meta; rules←test; kernel←meta; all←cli Cargo.toml deps; cargo tree checked in CI
tsp-test never depends on tsp-kernel/tsp-meta empirical guard stays independent of the proofs (dual guard) — checked by cargo tree
Every conformance/lang/*.lang has a proofs/*.proof (sound) or a broken/*.expect (rejected) conformance/manifest.toml completeness check in ci.yml
Each phase ## 12..## 25 maps to a named path (10.2–10.4 right column) BUILD_PLAN.md phase→path table; reviewed per session

11. Core Schema and Core Types

Normative data model. Every phase (## 12–## 25) builds against these EXACT signatures. Bodies omitted (lprompt RULE 10); ≤2-line illustrative snippets only. Greek written out (lambda-Pi, Pi, Gamma).

11.0 Cross-cutting invariants (apply to ALL types below)

Property Rule Rationale
Immutability Type, Term, KTerm, Judgment, Rule, Derivation are value types; once built, never mutated structural sharing + reasoning soundness; functions return NEW terms
Sharing Box<T> for recursive children; clone = shallow shell + deep Box walk; no Rc/Arc in default build de Bruijn terms small; cloning a subtree is O(size) but trees are bounded (test gen ≤ depth 12)
Identity equality is by VALUE: alpha_eq for Term, equal for KTerm; never pointer identity de Bruijn makes alpha-equality structural; kernel conversion is up to definitional equality
Concurrency all checkers (infer, check_deriv, infer_type, whnf, equal, step, check_progress, check_preservation) are PURE read-only functions of their args → Send + Sync, trivially parallel across terms/threads no global mutable state anywhere in tsp-term/tsp-rules/tsp-kernel/tsp-test
no_std tsp-term, tsp-syntax, tsp-kernel are #![no_std] + alloc (TCB has no std dependency surface) shrinks TCB; only tsp-cli is std
TCB membership INSIDE: tsp-kernel (KTerm, Sort, Ctx, infer_type, check_type, whnf, equal) + tsp-term::{shift, subst, alpha_eq} (the substitution lemma mirrors these). OUTSIDE: everything else is checked by the kernel gravest failure = kernel accepts ill-typed proof; keep TCB ≤ ~1500 LoC, audited
Derive set every core type #[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq, Eq)]; Atom also Hash; serde derives behind feature="serde" (off in TCB build) serde optional → no untrusted parser in TCB

Atom = interned symbol, pub struct Atom(u32) indexing a Vec<&str> symbol table held by tsp-syntax; Copy, Eq, Hash, O(1) compare. Used for sort names, judgment forms, rule names, base type names.

11.1 tsp-term — object terms + binding (PARTIALLY in TCB)

shift/subst/alpha_eq are TCB: the substitution lemma (Phase 7, ## 19) is the kernel-checked statement that subst is type-preserving; an off-by-one in shift breaks it.

/// Object-language types. Immutable; recursive children boxed.
pub enum Type {
    Base(Atom),                          // e.g. Bool, Nat — nullary base type
    Arrow(Box<Type>, Box<Type>),         // S -> T  (function)
    Prod(Box<Type>, Box<Type>),          // S * T   (Phase 10 extension)
    Sum(Box<Type>, Box<Type>),           // S + T   (Phase 10 extension)
    Ref(Box<Type>),                      // Ref T   (Phase 12 stretch: references + store typing)
    All(Box<Type>),                      // forall. T  (System F stretch; binder => uses TyVar/de Bruijn at type level)
    TyVar(usize),                        // de Bruijn type variable (only meaningful under All)
}

/// Object terms in DE BRUIJN form: no names, binders are positional.
/// Var(k) refers to the k-th enclosing binder (0 = innermost).
pub enum Term {
    Var(usize),                          // de Bruijn index (NOT a name)
    Lam(Type, Box<Term>),                // \: T. body  — binder; body is in an extended context
    App(Box<Term>, Box<Term>),
    TmTrue, TmFalse,
    If(Box<Term>, Box<Term>, Box<Term>), // if e1 then e2 else e3
    Zero, Succ(Box<Term>),               // Nat constructors; products/sums/refs ctors added in Phase 10/12
}

/// de Bruijn SHIFT: add `d` to every free Var index >= `cutoff`. TCB.
/// Walking under a Lam increments cutoff by 1. `d` is isize (can be -1 on subst).
/// Invariant: shift(t,0,c) == t; shift(shift(t,a,c),b,c) == shift(t,a+b,c).
pub fn shift(t: &Term, d: isize, cutoff: usize) -> Term;

/// CAPTURE-AVOIDING substitution [s/j]t: replace Var(j) by `s`, shifting `s`
/// under each binder it crosses. TCB. Capture-avoidance is AUTOMATIC given correct shift.
/// Beta uses subst(body, 0, shift(arg,1,0)) then shift(_, -1, 0) — see Phase 4.
pub fn subst(t: &Term, j: usize, s: &Term) -> Term;

/// Structural (de Bruijn) equality == alpha-equivalence. TCB.
/// Total, decidable; no name comparison needed because terms are nameless.
pub fn alpha_eq(a: &Term, b: &Term) -> bool;

/// Value predicate: Lam, TmTrue, TmFalse, Zero, Succ(v) where is_value(v), ... .
/// Drives `step` (a value is stuck = irreducible) and canonical_forms.
pub fn is_value(t: &Term) -> bool;
  • Ownership: caller owns &Term; all four fns RETURN owned Term (or bool); no interior mutability.
  • Closed-term predicate: is_closed(t) := max free index < 0 i.e. no Var(k) escapes its binders; required precondition for top-level typing |- e : T (empty context).
  • Serialization: Term/Type round-trip through tsp-syntax's object-term grammar (§11.2); de Bruijn ↔ named printing is a display concern (tsp-cli), never affects identity.
  • Pitfall: shift cutoff vs subst index off-by-one is THE classic bug; property tests (tsp-test) assert subst/shift algebraic laws; the substitution lemma (## 19) is the proof.

11.2 tsp-syntax — the metalanguage front end (NOT in TCB)

Parses a .lang file → LangDef and object-program text → Term. Untrusted: its output is re-checked downstream (check_deriv, kernel). Errors are diagnostics, never panics.

/// A complete object-language definition (the metalanguage's top-level result).
pub struct LangDef {
    pub sorts:     Vec<Atom>,            // declared sorts, e.g. Tm, Ty, Ctx
    pub grammar:   Vec<Production>,      // term + type grammar w/ binding annotations
    pub judgments: Vec<JudgmentForm>,    // judgment FORMS, e.g. Has-Type(Ctx, Tm, Ty)
    pub rules:     Vec<Rule>,            // inference rules (premises => conclusion)
}

/// One grammar production with binder annotations (which arg positions bind).
pub struct Production { pub sort: Atom, pub ctor: Atom, pub args: Vec<SortRef>, pub binders: Vec<BinderSpec> }

/// A judgment FORM (signature): name + the sorts of its argument positions.
pub struct JudgmentForm { pub form: Atom, pub arg_sorts: Vec<Atom> }

pub struct Lexer<'a> { /* byte cursor over &'a str; yields Token */ }
pub enum Token { /* Ident, LParen, Turnstile, Arrow, Colon, ..., Eof */ }
pub struct Parser<'a> { /* recursive-descent over Token stream */ }

pub struct ParseError { pub kind: ParseErrorKind, pub span: (usize, usize), pub msg: alloc::string::String }
pub enum ParseErrorKind {
    UnexpectedToken,                     // lexical/grammar mismatch
    UnknownSort,                         // reference to undeclared sort
    BadBinder,                           // binder annotation inconsistent w/ production arity
    MalformedRule,                       // premise/conclusion not a valid JudgmentForm instance
}
  • Ownership: Lexer<'a>/Parser<'a> borrow source &'a str; produced LangDef/Term are owned, source-independent (idents interned to Atom).
  • Mutation: parser has internal cursor mutation only; output values immutable.
  • Concurrency: parse one file per thread; Atom interner is the one shared mutable structure → single-threaded per LangDef build (or guarded), then read-only forever.
  • Serialization: .lang textual form IS the serialization of LangDef; pretty-printer is the inverse (round-trip fixture in ## 14).
  • Validation: parser checks sort/arity/binder well-formedness (UnknownSort/BadBinder/MalformedRule) BEFORE handing Rules to tsp-rules; deeper validity is check_deriv/kernel.

11.3 tsp-rules — inductive relations + executable checkers (NOT in TCB)

Typing Gamma |- e : T and small-step e --> e' as data (Rule) plus executable oracles (infer, step) and a derivation-tree validator (check_deriv). Executable checkers are CONVENIENCES; ground truth for proofs is the kernel.

/// An instance of a judgment form: form name + filled argument terms.
/// e.g. Has-Type with args [ctx-as-term, e, T-as-term].
pub struct Judgment { pub form: Atom, pub args: Vec<Term> }

/// An inference rule: name, premise judgments, one conclusion judgment.
///   premises
///   --------- (name)
///   conclusion
pub struct Rule { pub name: Atom, pub premises: Vec<Judgment>, pub conclusion: Judgment }

/// A DERIVATION TREE: which rule, the subderivations proving each premise,
/// and the concluded judgment. Immutable; children owned (Vec, not Box, since arity varies).
pub struct Derivation { pub rule: Atom, pub subderivations: Vec<Derivation>, pub conclusion: Judgment }

/// Validate a derivation tree against the rule set: every node's (rule, subderivations,
/// conclusion) must instantiate some Rule in `rules` (premises match children's conclusions,
/// modulo the substitution the rule schema requires). Result, never panic.
pub fn check_deriv(d: &Derivation, rules: &[Rule]) -> Result<(), DerivError>;
pub struct DerivError { pub at: Atom, pub kind: DerivErrorKind }   // NoSuchRule | ArityMismatch | PremiseMismatch | ConclusionMismatch

/// EXECUTABLE type checker (algorithmic STLC): infer the unique type of `e` in `ctx`,
/// or a TypeError. `check` = infer + alpha/structural compare. Total on well-formed input.
pub struct Context { /* de Bruijn list (Vec<Type>) of variable types; grows under Lam */ }
pub fn infer(ctx: &Context, e: &Term) -> Result<Type, TypeError>;
pub fn check(ctx: &Context, e: &Term, t: &Type) -> Result<(), TypeError>;
pub enum TypeError { Unbound(usize), Mismatch{ expected: Type, found: Type }, NotAFunction(Type), NotABool, /* ... */ }

/// SMALL-STEP stepper: one reduction (beta + congruence via evaluation contexts).
/// None => `e` is a value OR a STUCK term (progress says well-typed closed terms are never stuck).
pub fn step(e: &Term) -> Option<Term>;
  • Ownership: all inputs by &; infer returns owned Type; step returns owned Option<Term>; check_deriv borrows the whole tree + rule slice.
  • Context: a Vec<Type> indexed by de Bruijn level; pushed on entry to a Lam, popped on exit; threaded by value (cheap clone-on-extend) so checking is reentrant/parallel across subterms.
  • Mutation: none observable; Context extension produces a new logical scope (push/pop discipline, not aliased mutation).
  • Concurrency: infer/step/check_deriv pure → run over a corpus of terms in parallel (tsp-test does this).
  • Relationship to TCB: infer and step are algorithmic mirrors of the inductive relations the kernel reasons about; a discrepancy between infer and the kernel-encoded typing relation is itself a tested invariant (## 21, ## 23). The kernel — not infer — is authoritative.
  • Determinism: step is deterministic (single evaluation order); optional determinism lemma (## 16) states step(e) is unique.

11.4 tsp-kernel — the lambda-Pi proof kernel (THE TCB)

Trusted base. A Prop is a KType (= a KTerm of Sort::Type/Sort::Kind); a Proof is a KTerm that check_type accepts at that KType. Soundness of the WHOLE project reduces to soundness of these functions. Target: ≤ ~1500 LoC, no unsafe, no allocation in hot conversion paths beyond Box, exhaustive match on KTerm.

/// lambda-Pi (dependent) terms = types = proofs, all one syntactic category.
/// de Bruijn indices for variables. Immutable; recursive children boxed.
pub enum KTerm {
    Var(usize),                          // de Bruijn index into Ctx
    Sort(Sort),                          // a universe (Type or Kind)
    Pi(Box<KTerm>, Box<KTerm>),          // dependent function type: (x : A) -> B   (A = domain, B = codomain w/ x bound)
    Lam(Box<KTerm>, Box<KTerm>),         // \x : A. body   (A = domain annotation)
    App(Box<KTerm>, Box<KTerm>),         // f a
}

/// The two universes. Discipline: Type : Kind. (No Kind : ? — stratified, no Type:Type.)
pub enum Sort { Type, Kind }

/// Typing context: a STACK (de Bruijn list) of KTerm types, innermost last.
/// GROWS on entry to Pi/Lam, shrinks on exit. The one structure with stack lifetime.
pub struct Ctx { /* Vec<KTerm>; index i (from the right) types Var(i) */ }
impl Ctx {
    pub fn extend(&self, ty: KTerm) -> Ctx;     // push a binder type (returns extended; immutable view)
    pub fn lookup(&self, i: usize) -> Option<&KTerm>;
}

/// THE TRUSTED CHECKER. Infer (synthesize) the type of `t`, or KernelError.
/// Bidirectional: infer_type synthesizes; check_type checks against an expected type
/// via `equal`. Must be TOTAL on all KTerm inputs (terminate, never panic) and SOUND
/// (never assign a type a term does not have).
pub fn infer_type(ctx: &Ctx, t: &KTerm) -> Result<KTerm, KernelError>;
pub fn check_type(ctx: &Ctx, t: &KTerm, ty: &KTerm) -> Result<(), KernelError>;
pub enum KernelError { UnboundVar(usize), NotAFunction(KTerm), Mismatch{ expected: KTerm, found: KTerm },
                       NotASort(KTerm), IllSorted /* universe rule violated */ }

/// WEAK-HEAD NORMAL FORM: reduce only the head redex (beta) until the head ctor is
/// exposed; do NOT reduce under binders or in arguments. Used by `equal`.
pub fn whnf(t: &KTerm) -> KTerm;

/// DEFINITIONAL EQUALITY (conversion): are `a` and `b` equal up to beta (and optionally
/// eta)? Algorithm: whnf both, compare heads, recurse on subterms (extending ctx under binders).
/// This is what makes the kernel a CHECKER not a normalizer — types are compared up to `equal`.
pub fn equal(ctx: &Ctx, a: &KTerm, b: &KTerm) -> bool;
  • Ownership: every fn takes &Ctx + &KTerm, returns owned (KTerm / () / bool); Ctx::extend returns a new Ctx (logical push), so checking under a binder never mutates the caller's context → reentrant + parallel.
  • Mutation: Ctx is the ONLY thing that "grows", and only along the call stack (push on enter binder, the extended Ctx dies on return). No global state, no caches with mutation in the trusted path.
  • Concurrency: pure functions; verifying many proofs (tsp-meta lemma library) parallelizes across cores with zero synchronization.
  • Termination: infer_type recursion is structural on KTerm; whnf/equal terminate on WELL-TYPED inputs (kernel only trusts equality on terms it has typed); adversarial ill-typed KTerm must still NOT panic and SHOULD reject — fuzzed in ## 25.
  • Serialization: proof terms (KTerm) have a textual surface (the .lang proof-script grammar in tsp-syntax); the kernel consumes parsed KTerm only — the parser is OUTSIDE the TCB, so a parser bug can produce a wrong-but-rejected term, never an accepted false proof.
  • Load-bearing invariant: infer_type/check_type SOUNDNESS. A bug accepting an ill-typed KTerm silently validates a false theorem (gravest failure class). Hence TCB minimality + audit + fuzz.

11.5 tsp-meta — metatheory statements + proofs (NOT in TCB; PRODUCES TCB inputs)

Encodes object judgments as kernel inductive signatures (propositions-as-types) and bundles each theorem as a Proof { goal, term } where term : goal is verified by tsp-kernel. tsp-meta is untrusted: if it builds a bogus Proof, the kernel REJECTS it.

/// Build the PROPOSITION (a KType) for Progress over the given language:
///   forall e T. (|- e : T) -> (value(e) \/ exists e'. e --> e')
/// expressed in lambda-Pi (Pi for forall/implication; Sum/Sigma encoding for \/ and exists).
pub fn progress_statement(lang: &LangDef) -> KTerm;

/// Build the PROPOSITION for Preservation (subject reduction):
///   forall e e' T. (|- e : T) /\ (e --> e') -> (|- e' : T)
pub fn preservation_statement(lang: &LangDef) -> KTerm;

/// A theorem package: `term` is a proof KTerm that the kernel must accept at type `goal`.
/// VALID iff tsp_kernel::check_type(&Ctx::empty(), &p.term, &p.goal).is_ok().
pub struct Proof { pub goal: KTerm, pub term: KTerm }
  • Lemma library (## 19) returns Proofs for: canonical_forms, inversion, weakening, and the substitution lemma (Gamma,x:S |- e:T and Gamma |- v:S imply Gamma |- [v/x]e:T). The substitution lemma's proof term MIRRORS tsp-term::subst/shift exactly.
  • Preservation/Progress (## 20/## 21) consume those lemma Proofs as sub-derivations.
  • Ownership/concurrency: Proof is plain immutable data; statements/proofs are built independently → parallel; verification is the pure kernel call above.
  • Serialization: a Proof serializes as the goal .lang proposition + the proof-term surface syntax; tsp-cli check re-parses and re-verifies (trust nothing but the kernel).
  • TCB note: progress_statement/preservation_statement are statement builders — a bug here changes WHAT is proved, so they are reviewed as carefully as the kernel even though they are not literally in the TCB (a true proof of the wrong theorem is worthless). The theorem text is pinned in conformance fixtures (## 25).

11.6 tsp-test — Redex-style falsifier (NOT in TCB)

Fast empirical counterexample search complementing the proofs. The DUAL GUARD: a deliberately broken rule (## 24) must be caught by BOTH the kernel (proof fails) AND this tester (shrunk counterexample).

/// Size-bounded generator of WELL-TYPED object terms (by construction: builds a derivation,
/// reads off the term). Seeded for reproducibility. Bounded depth/size to ensure termination.
pub struct Generator { /* rng seed, size bound, target-type strategy */ }
impl Generator {
    pub fn well_typed(&mut self, ty: &Type, size: usize) -> Option<Term>;   // None if size budget exhausted
}

/// Outcome of a property run: how many cases passed, or a counterexample + which property failed.
pub enum TestResult {
    Pass(u64),                           // u64 = number of cases that passed
    CounterExample(Term, Witness),       // the offending term + Witness describing the failure
}
pub enum Witness {
    ProgressFailed{ stuck: Term },                       // closed well-typed term with step(e)=None and !is_value(e)
    PreservationFailed{ before: Term, after: Term, ty: Type },  // |-before:ty, before-->after, but !(|-after:ty)
}

/// Property oracles over a SINGLE term (used by the harness; combine with Generator + Shrinker).
pub fn check_progress(e: &Term) -> bool;       // is_value(e) || step(e).is_some()   (for closed, well-typed e)
pub fn check_preservation(e: &Term) -> bool;   // step(e) preserves infer(e) (when both defined)

/// Delta-debugging shrinker: given a counterexample, find a structurally minimal one
/// (fewer/smaller subterms) still violating the property. Reduces witness to human-auditable size.
pub struct Shrinker { /* candidate-reduction strategy over Term */ }
  • Ownership: Generator/Shrinker hold mutable RNG/strategy state (the only mutation in tsp-test); produced Terms + TestResult/Witness are immutable owned data.
  • Concurrency: each property run is independent given a distinct seed → embarrassingly parallel; aggregate Pass(n) counts; first CounterExample wins (then shrink single-threaded).
  • Determinism/serialization: seed + size fully reproduce a run; a CounterExample serializes via the object-term grammar so it can be replayed under tsp-cli test --seed.
  • Cross-validation invariant (## 23): on the SOUND language, check_progress/check_preservation ⇔ true for all generated terms AND the kernel proofs verify — agreement is asserted; divergence is a bug in one of the two guards.
